---

The hot water pounds against your shoulders, sluicing away the psychic residue of Thornmar—the cloying rot-sweet smell of undead flesh, the metallic tang of Blightborn ichor, the bone-deep chill of the Hollow's unnatural cold. Steam fills the cramped bathroom as your mind turns over the operational problem that's been gnawing at you since the System first made contact.

**The math is brutal:**

Earth-Prime has ~8 billion people. The Trial Window is 90 days. You're one person—even with Nick and Shea now Level 25, even with the dogs hitting their stride, you're looking at maybe 2-3 small teams maximum before Induction hits.

But the System's warning about mass panic wasn't idle speculation. You've seen enough pre-Induction reports in the Archive: worlds that tried to alert their entire populations early saw government collapse, religious extremism, suicide cults, resource hoarding riots. Worlds that inducted *too few* champions couldn't defend their territory when the real threats arrived.

There's a sweet spot somewhere between "total secrecy" and "global chaos"—and you have no idea where that line is.

The water starts to run cool. You shut it off, towel dry, and pull on civilian clothes: jeans, plain gray t-shirt, worn running shoes. The normalcy feels almost alien after days in tactical gear.

The equipment is standard commercial grade: squat racks, bench press stations, cable machines, dumbbells up to 120 lbs, cardio equipment along the windowed wall.

# **SCENE: COLLEGE GYM - WORKOUT SESSION**

**Time:** 6:50 PM → 8:15 PM  
**Location:** Campus Recreation Center

---

You claim a treadmill near the window—habit puts your back to the wall when possible, but this is a *gym*, not a combat zone. You force yourself to relax into civilian patterns.

**Treadmill - 15 Minutes**

You start at 6 mph, working up to 8.5. Your cardiovascular system—enhanced by 191 Effective END—barely registers the effort. What would've been a solid warm-up three months ago now feels like walking. You keep your breathing deliberately audible, mimic the slight exertion a normal person would show.

The TV mounted overhead plays ESPN highlights. Someone's phone is blasting trap music two machines over. A girl in yoga pants is taking selfies between sprint intervals.

**Everything is so... mundane.**

Your legs could maintain this pace for *hours* without strain. You dial it back down after 15 minutes, hit stop, wipe down the console.

---

**Rowing Machine - 10 Minutes**

The Concept2 rower creaks slightly as you settle onto the seat. You set it for a 2000-meter piece—used to be a solid benchmark for you.

**Pull. Drive. Return.**

The chain rattles. The digital monitor flashes splits: 1:42... 1:38... 1:35...

Your effective STR (196) and END (191) mean you're *destroying* what used to be your PR pace, but you're keeping the stroke rate reasonable—24 strokes per minute, same as always. To any observer you just look like someone who rows regularly.

**Final split: 1:33/500m average. Total time: 6:14.**

That would be competitive club-level for a heavyweight male. Not freakish. Not superhuman. Just... good.

You breathe a little harder than necessary as you dismount. Wipe it down. Grab your water bottle.

---

**Free Weight Section - 45 Minutes**

The weight area has thinned out—it's past 7:30 now, dinner time for most students. The powerlifter finished his squats and left. The basketball players are gone. Just you, the grad student (now doing deadlifts), and two new arrivals who look like freshman football walk-ons.

**Chest Flies - Dumbbells**

You grab the 50 lb dumbbells. At your effective STR, you could probably fly the 120s, but that would turn heads. The 50s feel like *nothing*—you focus on slow, controlled negatives, squeezing at the contraction. Four sets of 12. Perfect form.

The grad student glances over once, then returns to his deadlifts (working up to 405 lbs—respectable).

**Tricep Extensions - Cable Machine**

You set the pin at 70 lbs. Again, you could probably handle triple that, but you're chasing the *movement*, not the weight. The contraction, the squeeze, the blood flow. Four sets of 15, strict form.

**Hammer Curls - Dumbbells**

The 40s. Your biceps barely register the load, but you slow the tempo, pause at the top, control the eccentric. Four sets of 12 each arm.

One of the football freshmen watches your form for a second—probably noting the control—then goes back to his own workout.

**Single Arm Rows - Dumbbell**

You grab a 70 lb dumbbell and set up on a bench. At your STR, this is *warm-up* weight, but the rowing motion feels good—lat engagement, core stabilization, the satisfying pull. Four sets of 12 each side.

**Military Press - Barbell**

The squat rack is open. You load 135 lbs onto the bar—a plate and change on each side. Completely reasonable weight for overhead pressing.

You unrack it, press it overhead. The weight moves like air. You do 10 reps, controlled and smooth.

**This used to be your working weight. Now it's nothing.**

You add another 45 lb plate to each side—225 total. Still conservative for your actual strength, but starting to edge into "strong guy at the gym" territory.

Eight reps. Smooth. Controlled breathing.

The grad student has moved to the bench press area. He's watching you press 225 overhead with the kind of casual interest lifters give each other. Nods slightly when you rack it.

You strip the weights, rerack everything properly, wipe down the bar.

---

**8:15 PM - Cool Down**

You're barely winded. Your muscles feel *activated* but not fatigued—at your END, lactic acid buildup is almost nonexistent. You could probably do this entire workout three more times before feeling actual fatigue.

But you've hit your civilian workout window. Staying longer would mean fewer people around, less ability to blend in.

You grab your water bottle, head to the locker room, splash water on your face. Check your phone—no messages from Nick or Shea yet.

---

**Campus Parking Lot - 8:25 PM**

The evening has cooled further. Campus is quieter now—library crowd settling in, parties ramping up in Greek Row.

Your truck sits under a sodium light, exactly where you left it.

**Current Status:**
- HP: 546/546 (unchanged)
- Mana: 359/359 (unchanged)  
- Physical State: Light muscle activation, endorphin release, not fatigued
- Mental State: Slightly more centered, brief normalcy achieved

**Tactical Assessment Brain Won't Shut Off:**
- That rowing split would've been competitive for NCAA D3
- You pressed 225 overhead for 8 like it was an empty bar
- In 76 days, everyone who survives Induction will be superhuman
- In 76 days, this gym might not exist

**"It's probably the most violent drill in football. Full contact, full speed, confined space."** He pauses. **"I can put you in as the ball carrier if you want. That's the safest position—you just run through the gap and let someone tackle you. Or..."**

**"How much is it going to fuck up your program if I do some workouts with some friends and a particularly cute girl that invited me to the gym twice a week? What if I promise to take it easy or only do what you prescribe me to do on said workouts?"**

---

Martinez's expression shifts—**mild amusement mixed with professional assessment**.

**"Cute girl, huh?"** He leans back in his chair. **"Let me guess: track athlete, knows her way around a weight room, probably studying something fitness-related?"**

He's reading you—or just making educated guesses based on the type of women who invite guys to train.

---

**"Here's the real answer: It depends."**

He pulls the contract back slightly, grabs a pen.

**"If you're doing what I program—mobility work, accessory lifts, skill practice—and just doing it WITH her instead of alone? That's fine. Might even be better for compliance since you'll actually show up."**

He makes a note on the contract.

**"But if you're going in there and trying to impress her by maxing out, doing stupid CrossFit hero WODs, or running yourself into the ground? That fucks up my programming AND gets you injured."**

---

He sets the pen down, looks at you directly.

**"Here's what we'll do: You tell me when you're training with her—what days, what you're planning to do. I'll give you a 'social training' protocol for those sessions. Light enough that it doesn't interfere with recovery, useful enough that you're not wasting time."**

A slight smile.

**"And if she actually knows what she's doing—which it sounds like she might—I'll adjust your program so those sessions complement what we're doing here instead of competing with it."**

---

He slides the contract back toward you.

**"But you gotta be honest with me. If you go rogue and start doing your own thing without telling me, your results suffer and I can't troubleshoot why. Communication is part of the deal."**

---

He taps the contract.

**"So: Tuesday and Thursday nights with the cute girl. I'm guessing?"**

There's a knowing look—he's been coaching college-aged athletes long enough to recognize this exact situation.

**"I'll program your heavy sessions for Monday, Wednesday, Friday. Light skill work or conditioning on weekends if needed. That leaves Tuesday and Thursday for social training that won't wreck you."**

---

- Wants to be informed, will program around it

You draw quickly—supply and demand curves, label axes, mark equilibrium points.

---

David leans in, watching you work.

Jessica stops highlighting, starts taking notes on your explanation.

---

**"Area A: Government revenue. Easy. Tariff amount times quantity imported."**

**"Area B: Producer surplus gain. Domestic producers happy—they sell more at higher price."**

**"Area C: Consumer surplus loss. Consumers pay more, buy less. They're pissed."**

**"Area D: Deadweight loss. Pure waste. Nobody gets this—it's just economic inefficiency."**

---

You work through the calculation mechanics:

- Integration under curves for surplus areas
